I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. Is that the kind of weight change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut people at work have asked me if I’m sick because I guess I’m moving more slowly through ordinary tasks. And in meetings, sometimes someone asks me something simple and it takes me a moment to answer… longer than it should.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know a lot of this is just a difficult situation.\n\nBut I do keep replaying things in my head — every appointment I arranged or didn’t arrange fast enough, every conversation with my relatives where I got tense or defensive. And then it turns into this feeling like… maybe I’m just a selfish daughter. And not much better at work either, like I’m a useless employee on top of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh. I’m not saying it’s rational, exactly. It just feels like I deserve whatever happens sometimes. Is that the kind of guilt you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happened before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Before his decline? Um… not as much, I don’t think. It’s more tied to everything that’s been happening with him.\n\nI keep going back over decisions about his appointments — whether I pushed enough, or waited too long — and then every tense conversation with my relatives. And it sort of spirals into me feeling like I’m a selfish daughter, which… I know sounds extreme.\n\nAnd then I carry that into work too, like I’m just useless there as well. I don’t know if that answers your question properly. It’s not really old guilt, it’s more that this situation has made me feel like I deserve whatever happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t seem to let go of this feeling that I’m being punished. It sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, I know. It’s about my father declining, and I keep feeling like it’s my fault somehow—like I let it happen.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, and I try to believe her. But it still feels like some kind of judgment has already been made about me. I’m sorry, I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, sort of hollow feeling. Like the color has gone out of things.\n\nEven at work, sitting at my desk in HR, or when I’m looking at the garden beds at home, it’s like none of it really reaches me anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about what you could have done differently to prevent your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep replaying every appointment decision, like whether I pushed hard enough or asked the right questions, and then I go over the conversations with my relatives where things got tense. I know that may not be fair, and I’m not trying to sound like I think everything revolves around me… but I end up feeling like I was selfish, like I failed him.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads into everything. I’ll be at work and think, if I couldn’t even be a decent daughter, what good am I there? I’m sorry, that sounds harsh. But that’s where my mind goes sometimes… like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know people keep telling me it isn’t that simple, and I’m trying not to be irrational about it.\n\nBut I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, every time I maybe should’ve said more or done something differently… and the tense conversations with my relatives, too. It all turns into this feeling that I was selfish, like I was a bad daughter.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’m useless in other ways too, like even at work… if I failed at this, maybe I’m not good for much. I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. Is that the kind of weight change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut people at work have asked me if I’m sick because I guess I’m moving more slowly through ordinary tasks. And in meetings, sometimes someone asks me something simple and it takes me a moment to answer… longer than it should.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t seem to let go of this feeling that I’m being punished. It sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, I know. It’s about my father declining, and I keep feeling like it’s my fault somehow—like I let it happen.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, and I try to believe her. But it still feels like some kind of judgment has already been made about me. I’m sorry, I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, sort of hollow feeling. Like the color has gone out of things.\n\nEven at work, sitting at my desk in HR, or when I’m looking at the garden beds at home, it’s like none of it really reaches me anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about what you could have done differently to prevent your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep replaying every appointment decision, like whether I pushed hard enough or asked the right questions, and then I go over the conversations with my relatives where things got tense. I know that may not be fair, and I’m not trying to sound like I think everything revolves around me… but I end up feeling like I was selfish, like I failed him.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads into everything. I’ll be at work and think, if I couldn’t even be a decent daughter, what good am I there? I’m sorry, that sounds harsh. But that’s where my mind goes sometimes… like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know people keep telling me it isn’t that simple, and I’m trying not to be irrational about it.\n\nBut I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, every time I maybe should’ve said more or done something differently… and the tense conversations with my relatives, too. It all turns into this feeling that I was selfish, like I was a bad daughter.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’m useless in other ways too, like even at work… if I failed at this, maybe I’m not good for much. I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. Is that the kind of weight change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut people at work have asked me if I’m sick because I guess I’m moving more slowly through ordinary tasks. And in meetings, sometimes someone asks me something simple and it takes me a moment to answer… longer than it should.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know a lot of this is just a difficult situation.\n\nBut I do keep replaying things in my head — every appointment I arranged or didn’t arrange fast enough, every conversation with my relatives where I got tense or defensive. And then it turns into this feeling like… maybe I’m just a selfish daughter. And not much better at work either, like I’m a useless employee on top of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh. I’m not saying it’s rational, exactly. It just feels like I deserve whatever happens sometimes. Is that the kind of guilt you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happened before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Before his decline? Um… not as much, I don’t think. It’s more tied to everything that’s been happening with him.\n\nI keep going back over decisions about his appointments — whether I pushed enough, or waited too long — and then every tense conversation with my relatives. And it sort of spirals into me feeling like I’m a selfish daughter, which… I know sounds extreme.\n\nAnd then I carry that into work too, like I’m just useless there as well. I don’t know if that answers your question properly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t seem to let go of this feeling that I’m being punished. It sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, I know. It’s about my father declining, and I keep feeling like it’s my fault somehow—like I let it happen.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, and I try to believe her. But it still feels like some kind of judgment has already been made about me. I’m sorry, I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, sort of hollow feeling. Like the color has gone out of things.\n\nEven at work, sitting at my desk in HR, or when I’m looking at the garden beds at home, it’s like none of it really reaches me anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about what you could have done differently to prevent your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep replaying every appointment decision, like whether I pushed hard enough or asked the right questions, and then I go over the conversations with my relatives where things got tense. I know that may not be fair, and I’m not trying to sound like I think everything revolves around me… but I end up feeling like I was selfish, like I failed him.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads into everything. I’ll be at work and think, if I couldn’t even be a decent daughter, what good am I there? I’m sorry, that sounds harsh. But that’s where my mind goes sometimes… like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know people keep telling me it isn’t that simple, and I’m trying not to be irrational about it.\n\nBut I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, every time I maybe should’ve said more or done something differently… and the tense conversations with my relatives, too. It all turns into this feeling that I was selfish, like I was a bad daughter.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’m useless in other ways too, like even at work… if I failed at this, maybe I’m not good for much. I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. Is that the kind of weight change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut people at work have asked me if I’m sick because I guess I’m moving more slowly through ordinary tasks. And in meetings, sometimes someone asks me something simple and it takes me a moment to answer… longer than it should.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know a lot of this is just a difficult situation.\n\nBut I do keep replaying things in my head — every appointment I arranged or didn’t arrange fast enough, every conversation with my relatives where I got tense or defensive. I’m not saying it’s rational, exactly. It just feels like I deserve whatever happens sometimes. I don’t know if that answers your question properly. I guess… yes, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for literally every tiny thing, but even small decisions can start that feeling.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t seem to let go of this feeling that I’m being punished. It sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, I know. It’s about my father declining, and I keep feeling like it’s my fault somehow—like I let it happen.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, and I try to believe her. But it still feels like some kind of judgment has already been made about me. I’m sorry, I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, sort of hollow feeling. Like the color has gone out of things.\n\nEven at work, sitting at my desk in HR, or when I’m looking at the garden beds at home, it’s like none of it really reaches me anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about what you could have done differently to prevent your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep replaying every appointment decision, like whether I pushed hard enough or asked the right questions, and then I go over the conversations with my relatives where things got tense. I know that may not be fair, and I’m not trying to sound like I think everything revolves around me… but I end up feeling like I was selfish, like I failed him.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads into everything. I’ll be at work and think, if I couldn’t even be a decent daughter, what good am I there? I’m sorry, that sounds harsh. But that’s where my mind goes sometimes… like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know people keep telling me it isn’t that simple, and I’m trying not to be irrational about it.\n\nBut I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, every time I maybe should’ve said more or done something differently… and the tense conversations with my relatives, too. It all turns into this feeling that I was selfish, like I was a bad daughter.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’m useless in other ways too, like even at work… if I failed at this, maybe I’m not good for much. I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. Is that the kind of weight change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut people at work have asked me if I’m sick because I guess I’m moving more slowly through ordinary tasks. And in meetings, sometimes someone asks me something simple and it takes me a moment to answer… longer than it should.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know a lot of this is just a difficult situation.\n\nBut I do keep replaying things in my head — every appointment I arranged or didn’t arrange fast enough, every conversation with my relatives where I got tense or defensive. And then it turns into this feeling like… maybe I’m just a selfish daughter. And not much better at work either, like I’m a useless employee on top of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh. I’m not saying it’s rational, exactly. It just feels like I deserve whatever happens sometimes. Is that the kind of guilt you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happened before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Before his decline? Um… not as much, I don’t think. It’s more tied to everything that’s been happening with him.\n\nI keep going back over decisions about his appointments — whether I pushed enough, or waited too long — and then every tense conversation with my relatives. And it sort of spirals into me feeling like I’m a selfish daughter, which… I know sounds extreme.\n\nAnd then I carry that into work too, like I’m just useless there as well. I don’t know if that answers your question properly. It’s not really old guilt, it’s more that this situation has made me feel like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with your father or your relationship with him?\n\nPatient: Not really separate from him, I don’t think… or at least that’s where it starts.\n\nBut then it spreads, if that makes sense. I’ll go over every decision about his appointments, or every tense conversation with relatives, and somehow I end up thinking it means something bigger about me — like I’m a selfish daughter. And then at work I feel like I’m useless there too, like I can’t do anything right.\n\nI know that sounds… excessive. I’m not trying to blame everything on myself. It just starts to feel like I deserve whatever happens, even when I know that may not be fair. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your father's decline and your role in it?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and a couple of months, maybe. Since my father’s health and behavior started changing and I had to step in more with the caregiving.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just stress and that I was handling it, but that guilty feeling about my role in it has kind of been there since then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things even when you're alone and not thinking about your father?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for literally every tiny thing, but even small decisions can start that feeling.\n\nLike with my father’s appointments, I keep going over whether I chose the right time, said the right thing, pushed enough or too much. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t seem to let go of this feeling that I’m being punished. It sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, I know. It’s about my father declining, and I keep feeling like it’s my fault somehow—like I let it happen.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, and I try to believe her. But it still feels like some kind of judgment has already been made about me. I’m sorry, I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, sort of hollow feeling. Like the color has gone out of things.\n\nEven at work, sitting at my desk in HR, or when I’m looking at the garden beds at home, it’s like none of it really reaches me anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about what you could have done differently to prevent your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep replaying every appointment decision, like whether I pushed hard enough or asked the right questions, and then I go over the conversations with my relatives where things got tense. I know that may not be fair, and I’m not trying to sound like I think everything revolves around me… but I end up feeling like I was selfish, like I failed him.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads into everything. I’ll be at work and think, if I couldn’t even be a decent daughter, what good am I there? I’m sorry, that sounds harsh. But that’s where my mind goes sometimes… like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know people keep telling me it isn’t that simple, and I’m trying not to be irrational about it.\n\nBut I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, every time I maybe should’ve said more or done something differently… and the tense conversations with my relatives, too. It all turns into this feeling that I was selfish, like I was a bad daughter.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’m useless in other ways too, like even at work… if I failed at this, maybe I’m not good for much. I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. Is that the kind of weight change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut people at work have asked me if I’m sick because I guess I’m moving more slowly through ordinary tasks. And in meetings, sometimes someone asks me something simple and it takes me a moment to answer… longer than it should.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know a lot of this is just a difficult situation.\n\nBut I do keep replaying things in my head — every appointment I arranged or didn’t arrange fast enough, every conversation with my relatives where I got tense or defensive. I’ll go over every decision about his appointments, or every tense conversation with relatives, and somehow I end up thinking it means something bigger about me — like I’m a selfish daughter. And then at work I feel like I’m useless there too, like I can’t do anything right.\n\nI know that sounds… excessive. I’m not trying to blame everything on myself. It just starts to feel like I deserve whatever happens, even when I know that may not be fair. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know a lot of this is just a difficult situation.\n\nBut I do keep replaying things in my head — every appointment I arranged or didn’t arrange fast enough, every conversation with my relatives where I got tense or defensive. And then it turns into this feeling like… maybe I’m just a selfish daughter. And not much better at work either, like I’m a useless employee on top of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh. I’m not saying it’s rational, exactly. It just feels like I deserve whatever happens sometimes. Is that the kind of guilt you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happened before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Before his decline? Um… not as much, I don’t think. It’s more tied to everything that’s been happening with him.\n\nI keep going back over decisions about his appointments — whether I pushed enough, or waited too long — and then every tense conversation with my relatives. And it sort of spirals into me feeling like I’m a selfish daughter, which… I know sounds extreme.\n\nAnd then I carry that into work too, like I’m just useless there as well. I don’t know if that answers your question properly. It’s not really old guilt, it’s more that this situation has made me feel like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with your father or your relationship with him?\n\nPatient: Not really separate from him, I don’t think… or at least that’s where it starts.\n\nBut then it spreads, if that makes sense. I’ll go over every decision about his appointments, or every tense conversation with relatives, and somehow I end up thinking it means something bigger about me — like I’m a selfish daughter. And then at work I feel like I’m useless there too, like I can’t do anything right.\n\nI know that sounds… excessive. I’m not trying to blame everything on myself. It just starts to feel like I deserve whatever happens, even when I know that may not be fair. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your father's decline and your role in it?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and a couple of months, maybe. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t seem to let go of this feeling that I’m being punished. It sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, I know. It’s about my father declining, and I keep feeling like it’s my fault somehow—like I let it happen.\n\nMy sister keeps showing me the doctor’s notes and telling me I did everything I was supposed to do, and I try to believe her. But it still feels like some kind of judgment has already been made about me. I’m sorry, I’m not sure if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, sort of hollow feeling. Like the color has gone out of things.\n\nEven at work, sitting at my desk in HR, or when I’m looking at the garden beds at home, it’s like none of it really reaches me anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about what you could have done differently to prevent your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep replaying every appointment decision, like whether I pushed hard enough or asked the right questions, and then I go over the conversations with my relatives where things got tense. I know that may not be fair, and I’m not trying to sound like I think everything revolves around me… but I end up feeling like I was selfish, like I failed him.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads into everything. I’ll be at work and think, if I couldn’t even be a decent daughter, what good am I there? I’m sorry, that sounds harsh. But that’s where my mind goes sometimes… like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know people keep telling me it isn’t that simple, and I’m trying not to be irrational about it.\n\nBut I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, every time I maybe should’ve said more or done something differently… and the tense conversations with my relatives, too. It all turns into this feeling that I was selfish, like I was a bad daughter.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’m useless in other ways too, like even at work… if I failed at this, maybe I’m not good for much. I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. Is that the kind of weight change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or energetic you are compared to before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut people at work have asked me if I’m sick because I guess I’m moving more slowly through ordinary tasks. And in meetings, sometimes someone asks me something simple and it takes me a moment to answer… longer than it should.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. It’s just been hard to tell what’s normal under the circumstances, I guess.\n\nDoes that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things beyond your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know a lot of this is just a difficult situation.\n\nBut I do keep replaying things in my head — every appointment I arranged or didn’t arrange fast enough, every conversation with my relatives where I got tense or defensive. And then it turns into this feeling like… maybe I’m just a selfish daughter. And not much better at work either, like I’m a useless employee on top of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh. I’m not saying it’s rational, exactly. It just feels like I deserve whatever happens sometimes. Is that the kind of guilt you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happened before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Before his decline? Um… not as much, I don’t think. It’s more tied to everything that’s been happening with him.\n\nI keep going back over decisions about his appointments — whether I pushed enough, or waited too long — and then every tense conversation with my relatives. And it sort of spirals into me feeling like I’m a selfish daughter, which… I know sounds extreme.\n\nAnd then I carry that into work too, like I’m just useless there as well. I don’t know if that answers your question properly. It’s not really old guilt, it’s more that this situation has made me feel like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with your father or your relationship with him?\n\nPatient: Not really separate from him, I don’t think… or at least that’s where it starts.\n\nBut then it spreads, if that makes sense. I’ll go over every decision about his appointments, or every tense conversation with relatives, and somehow I end up thinking it means something bigger about me — like I’m a selfish daughter. And then at work I feel like I’m useless there too, like I can’t do anything right.\n\nI know that sounds… excessive. I’m not trying to blame everything on myself. It just starts to feel like I deserve whatever happens, even when I know that may not be fair. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your father's decline and your role in it?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and a couple of months, maybe. Since my father’s health and behavior started changing and I had to step in more with the caregiving.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just stress and that I was handling it, but that guilty feeling about my role in it has kind of been there since then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things even when you're alone and not thinking about your father?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it usually starts with my father, but even when I’m alone and not actively thinking about him, it’s like the feeling is still there.\n\nI’ll suddenly remember a decision about one of his appointments, or something tense I said to a relative, and then it turns into this bigger thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. And then I start thinking I’m useless at work too, like I can’t get anything right.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t want to make it seem more dramatic than it is, but sometimes it really feels like I deserve whatever happens. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty about things from your past, unrelated to your father's health?\n\nPatient: Not so much random things from long ago, I don’t think… it’s more that I replay things connected to my father and then it starts to feel like it says something about who I am.\n\nLike I’ll go over every decision about his appointments, or tense conversations with relatives, and I end up thinking I’m a selfish daughter. And then it kind of spills over into work too, like I’m useless there and deserve whatever happens.\n\nI know that may sound like I’m being too hard on myself… but yes, I do get stuck in that guilt.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about most things you do, even small, everyday decisions?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming myself for literally every tiny thing, but even small decisions can start that feeling.\n\nLike with my father’s appointments, I keep going over whether I chose the right time, said the right thing, pushed enough or too much. And then I replay tense conversations with my relatives. It ends up turning into this thought that I’m a selfish daughter… and then at work I feel like a useless employee too, like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nI know that sounds excessive. I’m sorry—I’m trying to answer it honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with your relationships or work?\n\nPatient: Maybe not completely separate… I’m trying to think.\n\nMost of the guilt does seem tied to my father, or my family, or then it spills into work. I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, and every tense conversation with my relatives, and then I end up feeling like I’m a selfish daughter. And somehow that turns into me feeling like I’m useless as an employee too, like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nSo… if you mean totally unrelated to relationships or work, I’m not sure as much. But the feeling spreads, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that are not related to your father, your family, or your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it really starts outside those areas, if I’m answering you correctly. It mostly comes back to my father’s appointments and whether I handled things right, and those arguments or tense talks with relatives.\n\nBut then once I’m in that place, it turns into this bigger feeling that I’m just… a selfish daughter, and then I start thinking I’m useless at work too, like I deserve whatever happens. So not exactly separate from my father or family or work, but it spreads into how I see myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that are not related to your father, your family, or your work?\n\nPatient: I think I may have answered this badly before… I’m sorry.\n\nIt’s not usually about random things, no. It mostly comes from the same places—my father’s appointments, whether I made the right decisions, and the tense conversations with my relatives. I keep replaying them and wondering what I should’ve done differently.\n\nAnd then it sort of turns into this bigger judgment about me… like I’m a selfish daughter, and then even at work I feel like I’m useless, like I deserve whatever happens. I know that may not be exactly what you’re asking, but that’s how it seems to go for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that are completely unrelated to your father, your family, or your work, such as hobbies or personal interests?\n\nPatient: Not really hobbies or personal interests, no… at least I don’t think that’s where it starts. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that I keep going back over every decision about my father’s appointments, and every tense conversation with my relatives. Then I end up feeling like I’m a selfish daughter. And once I’m feeling that way, it kind of spreads, and I start thinking I’m useless at work too… like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nSo I guess not completely unrelated things. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… sometimes. I mean, I try to enjoy whatever it is, but my mind drifts back to all the decisions about my father’s appointments, or things I said to relatives, and I start replaying it.

But it still feels like some kind of judgment has already been made about me. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, sort of hollow feeling. Like the color has gone out of things.\n\nEven at work, sitting at my desk in HR, or when I’m looking at the garden beds at home, it’s like none of it really reaches me anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about what you could have done differently to prevent your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nI keep replaying every appointment decision, like whether I pushed hard enough or asked the right questions, and then I go over the conversations with my relatives where things got tense. I know that may not be fair, and I’m not trying to sound like I think everything revolves around me… but I end up feeling like I was selfish, like I failed him.\n\nAnd then it sort of spreads into everything. I’ll be at work and think, if I couldn’t even be a decent daughter, what good am I there? I’m sorry, that sounds harsh. But that’s where my mind goes sometimes… like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I know people keep telling me it isn’t that simple, and I’m trying not to be irrational about it.\n\nBut I keep going back over every decision about his appointments, every time I maybe should’ve said more or done something differently… and the tense conversations with my relatives, too. It all turns into this feeling that I was selfish, like I was a bad daughter.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I’m useless in other ways too, like even at work… if I failed at this, maybe I’m not good for much. I know that probably sounds extreme. I’m sorry. But yes, I feel guilty… like I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your father's decline started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, anyway. I haven’t really been tracking it closely.\n\nMy appetite’s maybe been a bit off here and there, but I don’t want to overstate it. I don’t think my clothes are suddenly loose or anything like that. I don’t know if that counts as a change in energy, but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the things you enjoy doing since your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI still do some of the things I usually do, or at least I intend to. Maybe I’m less enthusiastic sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped enjoying everything. I’m not saying it’s rational, exactly. It just feels like I deserve whatever happens sometimes. Is that the kind of guilt you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happened before your father's decline?\n\nPatient: Before his decline? Um… not as much, I don’t think. It’s more tied to everything that’s been happening with him.\n\nI keep going back over decisions about his appointments — whether I pushed enough, or waited too long — and then every tense conversation with my relatives. I don’t know if that answers your question properly. It’s not really old guilt, it’s more that this situation has made me feel like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with your father or your relationship with him?\n\nPatient: Not really separate from him, I don’t think… or at least that’s where it starts.\n\nBut then it spreads, if that makes sense. I’ll go over every decision about his appointments, or every tense conversation with relatives, and somehow I end up thinking it means something bigger about me — like I’m a selfish daughter. And then at work I feel like I’m useless there too, like I can’t do anything right.\n\nI know that sounds… excessive. I’m not trying to blame everything on myself. It just starts to feel like I deserve whatever happens, even when I know that may not be fair. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your father's decline and your role in it?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year and a couple of months, maybe. Since my father’s health and behavior started changing and I had to step in more with the caregiving.\n\nAt first I told myself it was just stress and that I was handling it, but that guilty feeling about my role in it has kind of been there since then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about things even when you're alone and not thinking about your father?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it usually starts with my father, but even when I’m alone and not actively thinking about him, it’s like the feeling is still there.\n\nI’ll suddenly remember a decision about one of his appointments, or something tense I said to a relative, and then it turns into this bigger thought that I’m just… a selfish daughter. And then I start thinking I’m useless at work too, like I can’t get anything right.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t want to make it seem more dramatic than it is, but sometimes it really feels like I deserve whatever happens. It ends up turning into this thought that I’m a selfish daughter… and then at work I feel like a useless employee too, like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nI know that sounds excessive.
